## 1. What this is, and what its length does not mean

Rule R-e requires every converted path to preserve its error-to-response
mapping - same status codes, same error payload shapes. The migration
preserves three shared funnels verbatim, and that is **necessary but not
sufficient**: the funnels are reached from hundreds of local branches, each
branch decides *which* funnel its error reaches, and some reach none. This
document is one checkbox row per changed error edge, so the claim "the error
mappings survived" can be inspected rather than asserted.

**The row count is not a target and is not authoritative.** This run emitted 341 rows over 12 files. A different tree
yields a different number, and that is the intended behaviour: conversion
closes rows.

### What is in scope, and what deliberately is not

The analysed surface is the 12 files hapi itself invokes into: the ten
controllers, the named pre-handlers in `lib/util/helpers.js`, and the one
inline pre-handler in `config/api_routes.js`. Every one of them is read,
and a file with no row says so in its own section rather than being omitted.

Nothing else is reviewed. Rule R-a requires the diff to read as a runtime bump,
a hapi API migration, an async conversion and blocking-only dependency swaps -
so this is a record of the error edges **that conversion moves**, not a general
audit of the application's error handling. The internal callback modules keep
their callback interfaces under rule T-3, which puts the `await` boundary at the
lifecycle method rather than pushing it into the utility, so their internal
error edges are not conversion sites and carry no row here. Where a converted
handler awaits one of them, the edge that appears below is the handler's own.

### The sizing metric is not the row count

AAP 0.1.1.1 records **132 functions with an `err` parameter** in `lib/controllers/**`. That figure is a
**sizing metric and nothing else.** A crude grep over the baseline tree returns
133 rather than 132 depending on the pattern, which is itself the point:

- one callback can carry **zero** error dispositions - it never inspects `err` -
  or **several**: a guard, a log, and a late resolve;
- promise `.catch` handlers and synchronous `throw` statements add edges
  **entirely outside** that count;
- a `reply(` site is an error edge only when what it carries is an error, and at
  baseline 61 of the 202 sites carry success payloads.

This run counted 132 `err`-parameter functions in the ten controllers and
emitted 341 rows. Neither number derives from the other. Do not read the row
count as a coverage figure for the sizing metric, or the sizing metric as a
target for the row count.

## 2. The three shared funnels

Every row names the funnel its edge reaches. Line ranges below are read from
the analysed tree, not quoted from the plan, so they are correct for whichever
tree this document describes.

### Layer 1 - the handler catch-all, `lib/util/routeParser.js:578-589`

Logs `err.stack` when present, else `String(err)`, and returns
`Boom.badImplementation(err.message || String(err))` - so **500**. After the
async conversion this becomes structural: a rejecting `async` handler lands
here without anything having to call it.

Two properties of this block decide many rows below.

1. **The `if (err)` guard at `:579` is preserved.** A falsy throw satisfies the `catch` but
   fails the guard, so the block produces **no return**, and hapi's toolkit
   converts that `undefined` into the same status with a different message.
2. **It does not test `isBoom`.** A `throw Boom.notFound()` inside a
   handler is therefore answered **500** with Boom's hidden 5xx body, not 404,
   while `return Boom.notFound()` is answered 404. Same value, different verb,
   different status. Rows distinguish the two.

### Layer 2 - `request.fail`, `lib/util/routeParser.js:482-514`

Logs at info level, then selects one of three responses:

- negotiated `html` **and** a configured `fail.redirect`: flashes `failure` (`:490`),
  **interpolates `fail.redirect` in place** (`:491`) - the cross-request state leak,
  because the object being mutated was captured once at parse time - flashes
  `payload` and `query`, and redirects;
- negotiated `html`, a configured `fail.html` and no `.json` extension on the
  path: renders that view after `addUserContext`;
- otherwise: defaults the payload to `{}`, attaches `json.flash`, and returns
  `h.response(json)`.

None of those three is a Boom, so a Layer 2 response takes Layer 3's non-Boom
branch and **does** receive the cache headers.

The in-place interpolation is the **cross-request state leak**: the `fail`
object was captured once at parse time and the handler closure holds it by
reference, so the assignment mutates the long-lived object and a later request
to the same route redirects to the earlier request's target. It is catalogued,
with its measurement and its blast radius, in `docs/preserved-quirks.md` section 3.
R-d requires it: rows on this funnel preserve the mutation rather than
repairing it.

### Layer 3 - `onPreResponse`, `app.js:152-201`

For a Boom response it computes `isApiRequest` at `:161` from three tests: a path
beginning `/api/`, an `Accept` containing `application/json`, or a path
beginning `/partials/`.

**Branch order matters, and the code's shape misleads on it.** For a browser
HTML request the extension **returns immediately** on 401 (`h.redirect('/login')`
with `.takeover()` at `:172`), on 404, on 403 and on any status >= 500 -
**before** the header assignments that begin at `:182`. So:

- `Cache-Control`, `Pragma`, `Expires` and the conditional `X-Frame-Options`
  reach **API/JSON error responses and non-Boom responses only** (the non-Boom branch re-applies them at `:191`);
- they do **not** reach the rendered HTML error pages;
- a Boom status outside that set - a **400**, for instance - falls through the
  early returns and **does** receive them.

This is a correction R-e forced on an earlier reading of the code's shape. It
is stated here so nobody re-derives it wrongly from the source order.

`X-Frame-Options: deny` applies only to the 5 paths at `config/default.yaml:353-358`: `/`, `/login`, `/signup`, `/contact`, `/educators`.

### Not a funnel, but decisive: the pre-handler shim, `lib/util/routeParser.js:71-237`

Pre-handlers are not invoked by the handler wrapper, so **their errors never
reach Layer 1**. They go through `convertPreHandlers`, whose `fakeReply`:

- **rejects** on an `isBoom` value, so that Boom keeps its own status and
  reaches Layer 3 through hapi's own lifecycle error handling;
- **resolves** on anything else, *including a plain `Error`*. So `reply(err)`
  in a pre-handler with a non-Boom error resolves the pre-handler with the
  error object as its assigned value: the request continues, `request.pre.<assign>`
  holds an `Error`, and **no error response is produced at all**.

A synchronous `throw` inside a pre-handler is caught by the shim and rejected,
so a thrown Boom keeps its status there while the same statement in a handler
would be flattened to 500 by Layer 1. Rows on the two surfaces read
differently for that reason.

The shim is written twice - once for the object pre-handler form and once for
the bare-function form - with a `fakeReply` at `:87` and `:142`. Both behave identically for
the purposes above.

## 3. Two dispositions a mechanical conversion changes silently

These two are called out because converting them correctly looks like
converting them wrongly. Both appear as row dispositions below.

### log-and-continue

The handler logs and **returns nothing**, so the surrounding flow continues and
this edge produces no response. Mechanically converting such a handler into a
rejection - or into a returned Boom - turns an edge that produces no response
into one that does, and changes what a client receives from whatever else
settles the request into a 500.

The clearest instance is the streaming asset fetch, whose `error` listener only
`console.log`s. Two measured details belong with it, because they are different
failure modes with different outcomes:

- a **refused connection** emits `error` and **not** `end`, so the upload
  continuation never runs, **nothing is uploaded at all**, and the request is
  left **unsettled**. An earlier reading of this site had it uploading partial
  bytes; that was wrong, and the distinction matters because the two failure
  modes need different fixtures and produce different observable results;
- a **mid-stream failure after `response`** may still reach `end`, so *there*
  the continuation does run, on a partial file.

Both are baseline behaviour and both are preserved.

### resolve-on-later-callback

The response is produced inside a continuation-passing callback, so it settles
only when that operation calls back, and what a client receives today is
whichever settlement wins. These must **not** be collapsed into an earlier
`await`: rule T-3 puts the await boundary at the call site, and moving the
settlement earlier can change the response itself. Where such a callback also
declares no error parameter, the operation's own failure is dropped, and the row
says so.

## 4. How to read a row

```
  ... - [ ] `<file>:<line>` - <Disposition> - <Funnel> - <Shape>
  ...       Carrier <reachable code path> - Routes <bound routes> - Source <the line>
  ...       Target: <the outcome to preserve, ending in Side effects and Timing>
  ...       Note: <a measured detail, when there is one>
  ...       See also: <the sibling document that catalogues this edge>
```

A real row is a line beginning `- [ ] ` at column 0, and its continuation
lines are indented six spaces and begin `Carrier `, `Target: `, `Note: ` or
`See also: `. Every line of the sketch above carries a `... ` prefix that a
real row does not, so counting lines that begin `- [ ] ` at column 0 returns
the row count this document states and nothing else - the sketch does not
inflate it by one.

Nothing machine-reads this document. `test/parity/capture.js` carries the
corresponding failure-path scenarios by hand, in its `error-edge.*` groups,
and `test/parity/replay.js` cites this checklist as what supplies the failure
paths that one-minimal-request-per-route cannot reach. The row format is
stable and grep-friendly so that a reviewer, and a future consumer, can both
work from it.

**Disposition** is the closed vocabulary of AAP 0.6.3 and every row carries
exactly one value from it:

- `calls request.fail locally`
- `calls reply(err)`
- `returns or throws a Boom`
- `logs and continues`
- `swallows silently`
- `resolves on a later callback`

**Shape** carries the sub-shape that six values cannot express - `reply(err)`
with no return, a bare `.catch(request.fail)` reference, a synchronous throw, a
CPS callback boundary. Shape is descriptive and open; Disposition is closed.

**Funnel** is `Layer 1`, `Layer 2`, `Layer 3` or `none`. `none` is a legitimate and
important value: an edge that produces no response at all is the edge most
likely to be given one by accident.

**Target** states the outcome to **preserve** - status, payload or redirect,
side effects, timing. **No row proposes a fix.** R-d prohibits improvements, so
where the baseline outcome is a defect the row requires the defect.

**Carrier** is the reachable code path - the exported handler or pre-handler
the edge sits in - and **Routes** are the literal route declarations that bind
it. Every row carries a carrier, so every row is drivable; see section 9.

**See also** appears on a row whose edge is also catalogued elsewhere, and is
the only form of duplication this document permits.

### What this document does not own

Three documents describe the same edges from different angles, and each claim
belongs to exactly one of them:

| Document | Owns |
|---|---|
| this document | the per-edge status, payload or redirect, side effects and timing, and the funnel each edge must still reach |
| `docs/preserved-quirks.md` | why a baseline defect is kept, its measurement, and the two approved deviations |
| `docs/conversion-inventory.md` | the per-site return/await disposition for every handler body the conversion touches |

So a row states the outcome to preserve and points at the quirk catalogue for
the reasoning; it does not restate it. Every row whose Target carries the
return-discipline sentence is also a conversion site, and the call sites where
a missing `return` is what makes the conversion decisive carry an explicit
`See also` to `docs/conversion-inventory.md`.

classified every one of them as carrying a non-error value: the file was
analysed, not skipped.

      Target: The error is absorbed: no response, no rethrow, no log. Funnel: none. Preserve the swallow exactly - R-d prohibits repairing it, and a caller that today proceeds on a failed operation must keep proceeding. Side effects: none at all - no log, no response, no rethrow, no flash. The error value is discarded where it is received, and R-d requires that it keep being discarded. Timing: deferred. The handler is registered synchronously while the chain at line 43 is built, and runs only when that chain rejects - a microtask at the earliest, and after the handler body has already returned. The chain is assigned to `promise` at that statement rather than returned, so whether anything waits for the rejection is decided where `promise` is consumed - preserve that consumption point, and do not move the settlement to this one.

      Target: Layer 2. request.fail logs its argument at info level, then selects one of three responses: with a negotiated `html` type and a configured `fail.redirect` it flashes `failure`, interpolates `fail.redirect` IN PLACE on the parse-time route object - the cross-request leak - flashes `payload` and `query`, and redirects; with `html`, a configured `fail.html` and no `.json` extension on the path it renders that view through addUserContext; otherwise it returns h.response(json) with `json.flash` attached. Preserve the branch selection, both flash writes, the status and the body. The response is not a Boom, so Layer 3 takes its `else if (response.header)` branch and applies Cache-Control, Pragma and Expires. Under the shim the call's return value is discarded and the deferred promise carries the response, so the converted body must RETURN this value exactly once on this path - a returned-but-unawaited chain and an awaited-but-unreturned one both pass a signature count and both break this edge. This call site has no `return`, which is immaterial under the shim and decisive after conversion. Side effects: request.fail logs its argument at info level when one is present; on the `html` + `fail.redirect` branch it writes the `failure` flash (only when a payload was passed), mutates `fail.redirect` IN PLACE on the object captured at parse time, and writes the `payload` and `query` flashes; on the other two branches it calls `request.yar.flash()` with no arguments, which READS AND CLEARS the accumulated flash into the response body. Preserve exactly those writes - no more, and none dropped. The in-place mutation is the cross-request leak catalogued in docs/preserved-quirks.md section 3 and is required, not repaired. Timing: synchronous, on the handler body's own stack, before it returns.
      See also: docs/conversion-inventory.md - this call site has no `return`, so the per-site return/await disposition is tracked there as well as here

      Target: Layer 2. request.fail logs its argument at info level, then selects one of three responses: with a negotiated `html` type and a configured `fail.redirect` it flashes `failure`, interpolates `fail.redirect` IN PLACE on the parse-time route object - the cross-request leak - flashes `payload` and `query`, and redirects; with `html`, a configured `fail.html` and no `.json` extension on the path it renders that view through addUserContext; otherwise it returns h.response(json) with `json.flash` attached. Preserve the branch selection, both flash writes, the status and the body. The response is not a Boom, so Layer 3 takes its `else if (response.header)` branch and applies Cache-Control, Pragma and Expires. Under the shim the call's return value is discarded and the deferred promise carries the response, so the converted body must RETURN this value exactly once on this path - a returned-but-unawaited chain and an awaited-but-unreturned one both pass a signature count and both break this edge. Timing: synchronous, on the handler body's own stack, before it returns.

      Target: The shim's reply() selects on the runtime value and all three outcomes are observable: an isBoom value resolves unchanged with its own status; any other Error becomes Boom.badImplementation(err.message), so 500 with the 5xx message Boom hides from the client; a value that is neither is wrapped by h.response(value) and served 200 with the value as the body. Preserve the selection itself, not just the common case. Under the shim the call's return value is discarded and the deferred promise carries the response, so the converted body must RETURN this value exactly once on this path - a returned-but-unawaited chain and an awaited-but-unreturned one both pass a signature count and both break this edge. Side effects: the shim resolves its deferred promise with this value, which is the mechanism the conversion removes and the only effect this edge has beyond the response. No flash, session or database write happens here. Timing: asynchronous, in the chain step at line 557, so it settles a microtask after the handler body has run. The chain is NEITHER returned nor awaited, so nothing waits for it today and nothing may start waiting for it.

- [ ] `lib/controllers/users.js:589` - **calls request.fail locally** - Layer 2 - request.fail()
      Carrier `users.assetUploadFromURL` (route handler) - Routes `POST /api/users/assetFromURL` - Source `if (!requestUrl.protocol) return request.fail();`
      Target: Layer 2. request.fail logs its argument at info level, then selects one of three responses: with a negotiated `html` type and a configured `fail.redirect` it flashes `failure`, interpolates `fail.redirect` IN PLACE on the parse-time route object - the cross-request leak - flashes `payload` and `query`, and redirects; with `html`, a configured `fail.html` and no `.json` extension on the path it renders that view through addUserContext; otherwise it returns h.response(json) with `json.flash` attached. Preserve the branch selection, both flash writes, the status and the body. The response is not a Boom, so Layer 3 takes its `else if (response.header)` branch and applies Cache-Control, Pragma and Expires. Under the shim the call's return value is discarded and the deferred promise carries the response, so the converted body must RETURN this value exactly once on this path - a returned-but-unawaited chain and an awaited-but-unreturned one both pass a signature count and both break this edge. Side effects: request.fail logs its argument at info level when one is present; on the `html` + `fail.redirect` branch it writes the `failure` flash (only when a payload was passed), mutates `fail.redirect` IN PLACE on the object captured at parse time, and writes the `payload` and `query` flashes; on the other two branches it calls `request.yar.flash()` with no arguments, which READS AND CLEARS the accumulated flash into the response body. Preserve exactly those writes - no more, and none dropped. The in-place mutation is the cross-request leak catalogued in docs/preserved-quirks.md section 3 and is required, not repaired. Timing: synchronous, on the handler body's own stack, before it returns.
- [ ] `lib/controllers/users.js:596-598` - **logs and continues** - none - .on('error') handler - logs only
      Carrier `users.assetUploadFromURL` (route handler) - Routes `POST /api/users/assetFromURL` - Source `.on('error', function(err) {`
      Target: No response is produced here and nothing is thrown: the error is logged via `console.log` and control continues. Funnel: none - and that is the point. Preserve the continuation. Converting a log-and-continue branch into a rejection or a returned Boom turns an edge that produces no response into one that does, changes the status a client sees from whatever else settles the request to a 500, and is exactly the silent conversion R-e is written to catch. Side effects: the `console.log` call and nothing else - no flash, no session write, no response, no rethrow. A conversion that adds any of those changes this edge. Timing: when the stream emits `error`, which is not awaited by anything: for a refused connection it fires INSTEAD of `end`, and for a mid-stream failure it fires after `response`. The handler body has already returned by then, so the settlement time of the request is decided by whichever listener runs - or, when none does, not at all. Preserve that, including the case where nothing settles.
      Note: Sibling listeners on the same stream: `response`, `end`. A refused connection emits `error` and NOT `end`, so the `end` handler never runs and the request is left unsettled; a mid-stream failure after `response` may still reach `end`. Both outcomes are baseline behaviour and both are preserved.
      See also: docs/preserved-quirks.md section 8.1 - the streaming asset fetch's two failure modes

      Target: The response is produced inside the callback passed to `FileUtil.uploadUserAsset` (`request.fail`, `request.success`), so it settles only when that operation calls back, and what a client receives today is whichever settlement wins. Preserve the ordering: rule T-3 puts the await boundary at this call site, and the response must still be produced after the callback would have run. Collapsing this into an earlier await changes which settle wins and can change the response itself. Status and payload unchanged. Side effects: whatever the callback body performs before it settles, plus the effects of the settling calls `request.fail`, `request.success` itself - for `request.fail` that includes its flash writes. The deferred promise is resolved at that point and not before. Timing: deferred. The response is built only when `FileUtil.uploadUserAsset` invokes its callback, so the request settles then and not when the handler body returns. Rule T-3 puts the `await` boundary at this call site; moving the settlement earlier changes which settle wins.

Side effects: on the resolving path the pre-handler's assigned value becomes the value passed here, so `request.pre.<assign>` holds it for every later stage that reads it; on the rejecting path nothing is assigned. No session or database write happens at this edge. Timing: inside the callback `Trinket.findByOwnerAndSlug` invokes, off the handler's own stack, so it runs after the handler body has already returned.

## 9. Drivability

`test/parity/capture.js`'s failure-path scenarios are derived from these rows -
by hand, in its `error-edge.*` groups, not by parsing this file - and
`test/parity/replay.js` cites this checklist as what supplies the failure paths
the success sweep cannot reach. A row nothing can drive is not a testable
edge, so every row names either a bound route or the code path that reaches
it.

- Rows naming at least one route declaration: **315** of 341.
- Rows naming a reachable code path (their carrier): **341** of 341.
- Rows naming neither: **0**.

Route bindings are read from the `route :` declarations in `config/routes.js` and `config/api_routes.js`.
`config/routes.js` expands a subset of those per language at parse time, so the
registered surface is larger than the declarations named here; the expanded
route manifest belongs to `test/parity/manifest.js`. A declaration built by
concatenation is read through the concatenation and rendered with its
non-literal operands as `<name>`, so `GET /<lang>/{shortCode}` is a template
standing for one registered route per configured language - not a path with a
`lang` parameter.

A row whose carrier names no declaration is reached **through its carrier**, and
there are two such cases: a module-local function called by a routed handler,
marked `(module-local)` or `(internal)` in the carrier field, and an export
with no route reference at all. Both are driven by driving the routed caller,
or - for an unrouted export - by calling it directly.

| Carrier surface | Rows |
|---|---:|
| route handler | 314 |
| pre-handler | 27 |
